[trialAccount t18480515-1435]
1435. ELIZA THOMPSON and JOSEPH ROBIN ON , unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MR. DAWSON conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZA COX. On 22nd April, about seven in the evening, Thompson came to my mistress's shop for a halfpenny-worth of thread—I served her—she gave me a fourpenny-piece—I put it into the till, and gave her 3 1/2 d. change—she went away—there were half-crowns and shillings in the till, but no small money—I am sure there was no fourpenny-piece—after she was gone Troson came in—I went to the till and gave him the fourpenny-piece I re-ceived from Thompson—I identify it by a mark on it—no one bad been is the shop from the time Thompson came till Troson came
JOHN LOCK . On 22nd April, between six and seven in the evening, Thompson came to my shop for a pennyworth of tea—my wife served her—a man in private clothes at the door beckoned to me—Thompson gave my wife a fourpenny-piece—she laid it on the counter—I went round and took it up—it was bad—I said so—I bit it—Thompson said she did not know it, or something to that effect—soon afterwards the officer brought Robinson in.
MRS. LOCK. I served Thompson—what my husband has stated is true.
THOMAS TROSON ( policeman, M 337.) On 22nd April, between six and seven in the evening, I was on duty in plain clothes, in Union-street, South-wark—I saw the prisoners in company and followed them—Thompson went into Cox's shop—Robinson stood in the street, in sight of the shop—Thompson came out and gave something to Robinson, and they went on
together—I went in and received this fourpenny-piece—I them followed them to Mr. Lock's—I took Robinson—he threw this fourpenny-piece from his left hand, and said he had not been pitching —I took him into Mr. Lock's shop, and found on him 5 s. 2 1/2 d. in coppers, 2 s. in silver, and twenty-five packets of mustard, tea, pepper, and other things.
THOMAS WEST ( policeman, M 249.) I was with Troson on 22nd April, and saw the prisoners in Union-street—Robinson passed something to Thomp-son, about four yards from Cox's shop—Thompson entered the shop—Robinson passed the door—Thompson came out, joined Robinson, and gave him something, which he put into his coat-tail pocket—they then walked on to the New-cut—Thompson went into Mr. Lock's—Robinson waited outside, about fifteen yards off—I got this fourpenny-piece from Mr. Lock's.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL. I am inspector of coin to the Mint—these three fourpenny-pieces are counterfeit—two of them are cast in the same mould.
THOMPSON — GUILTY. Aged 20.
ROBINSON — GUILTY. Aged 18.
Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18480515-1436]
1436. ANN PUMPHERY and ANN MILLER , for a like offence.
MESSRS. ROBINSON and SCRIVEN conducted the Prosecution.
MARY ANN POWELL. I am apprentice to Mr. Kingsman, a linen-draper of Westminster-bridge-road. On 5th May, the prisoners came to the shop—Pumphery asked for a pennyworth of black tape—I served her—she gave me a sixpence—I gave it to the cashier—he took it away—I saw no more of it.
THOMAS GROOBY. I am cashier at Mr. Kingsman's. I took the sixpence from Miss Powell—I gave it to Mr. Kingsman—a policeman was sent for—he took two other sixpences from pumphery's hand—she tried to swallow them.
WILLIAM KINGSMAN. My cashier brought me a bad sixpence, on 5th May—I took it, and walked down to the end of the shop, where the prisoners stood side by side—Miss Powell said pumphery gave it her—I asked Pumphery where she got it, and whether she had any more—she said, "No." and she did not know it was bad—Miller said, "If I had known you wasted a penny I could have lent it you"—a policeman was sent for—he said to Pumphery, "What have you got in your hand"—she was trying to get it to her mouth—he seized her hand—she used very abusive language, and struggled very much—Miller was about to pull the policeman away—another policeman came in, and got two sixpences from Pumphery's hand—I marked the sixpence I had, and gave it to the officer.
DENNIS MURPHY ( policeman, P 102). I produce two sixpences which I took from Pumphery's hand, and one which I got from Mr. Kingsman.
MARY HEARN. I searched the prisoners at the station—Pumphery asked to go to the water-closet—I saw something like money in her hand—she threw it down—I pointed it out to the officer.
----( policeman, L 48). I found this sixpences in the water-closet.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL. These four sixpences are counterfeit—two of them have been cast in one mould.
PUMPHREY — GUILTY. Aged 23.— Confined Six Months.
MILLER — NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18480515-1437]
1437. ANN CURTIN and ELIZABETH SMITH , for a like offence.
JANE WALTON. I am servant to Mr. Robinson, of the Red Lion. On 14th April the prisoners came for half-a-quartern of gin—Curtin offered me a bad sixpence—I bent it—the prisoners were taken to the station, but were allowed to go away—I gave the sixpence to Wright.
ELIZABETH HUNT. I am barmaid to Mr. Brown. On 14th April the prisoners came—Curtin asked for some gin—she paid a fourpenny-piece—I took it up—it was bad—I bent it and gave it her back—Wright came in and took it from her hand.
JOHN WRIGHT ( policeman, M 63). I produce two sixpences—I got one from Jane Walton, and the other and two fourpenny-pieces I took from Curtin's hand.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL. These sixpences are counterfeit—these two fourpenny-pieces are coutnerfeit, and from the same mould.
Smith's Defence. I met this woman, who asked me to have something to drink—I said I had no money—the policeman laid hold of her hand and threw her on the ground—I said, "Don't hurt her," and they laid hold of me.
CURTIN — GUILTY. Aged 31.— Confined Nine Months.
SMITH — NOT GUILTY.

1439. JAMES M'LOCHLIN , breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Charles knight, at Rotherhithe , and stealing 1 pump box, 1 flanch, and 1 swivel, value 2 l. 3 s., his goods.
CHARLES KNIGHT. I am a plumber, at Rotherhithe-street. On 26th Feb., about one o'clock, I left my shop—I returned in ten minutes and found the window broken and a brass box, a patent box, and a swivel gone—they were mine—it is my dwelling-house, in Rotherhithe parish.
THOMAS GREENSHIELD. On 26th Feb. I saw the prisoner with some brass under his apron—he gave the box of a pump to another person, who has been convicted, about three yards from the shop—I said I would go and tell—Carter afterwards sent me into a public-house—I saw the prisoner—he asked what I wanted—I went to tell Carter, and he got out at the back door.
RICHARD DRYDEN. On 26th Feb. I found this article lying at the King and Queen, under a boom—I kept it, and found the owner.
THOMAS GREENSHIELD re-examined. This is like what I saw.
JOHN CARTHY ( policeman). I went to take the prisoner at a public-house in Rotherhithe—I sent Greenshield in to see if he ws there—he came out, and the prisoner escaped the back way.
GUILTY. Aged 20.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18480515-1440]
1440. OWEN SHEEN , was indicted for embezzlement.
THOMAS BOORAH. I am a dealer. An order for goods had been given to the prisoner in the name of Mansfield—he brought them on Friday, 24th March, and I paid him 2 l.—he gave me this receipt—it is signed "Mansfield"—he represented himself as Mansfield—on 27th March I paid him 3 l. 11 s. 4 d.—he represented himself as Mansfield, a nd signed the receipt Mansfield.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. You gave him the order for himself? A. He brought the card—I gave him the order.
THOMAS MANSFIELD. I employed the prisoner—I gave him the goods to go to Mr. Boorah—I authorised him to receive the money—he never paid me—I authorised him to receive money once before—on the Friday he received 2 l., and never accounted for it—on the Saturday he came, and had 13 s. 6 d. for his wages—I found him in a public-house tipsy—he swore he never received it.
Cross-examined. Q. You did not know he had received this 2 l.? A. No—he was paid according to what he did—I gave him half-a-crown for the job—I did not go to either of the customers before the money was received—I gave him the goods to carry out with another man—the money was to be paid to him—I went with him to the house—I remained ouside—he came out, and said Mr. Boorah was not in the way—I did not go in; they did not know me, and would not pay me—the prisoner had not known them before—he told me to stay outside—he received the order, and was to execute it—he had not been at work on the other transaction above a fortnight—I am sure the goods were mine.
GUILTY. Aged 49.— Confined Two Months.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18480515-1441]
1441. JAMES DOWDING and JAMES HILLESLEY , stealing 18lbs. weight of copper, value 8 s.; the goods of William John Loat, their master.
MR. PRENDERGAST conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM JOHN LOAT. I am a builder, at Clapham—Hillesley was my wagoner four or five months, and Dowding my gardener and odd-man. On 30th March I directed Hillesley to take the cart to Nine Elms; and on 6th April, in consequence of something I heard, I went to a stable on my premises, and found a piece of copper, worth 7 s. or 8 s.—I had not missed it; it had been in a closet or cupboard, in a shed, which was always locked—the padlock appeared locked and perfect—it was fastened by a secrew-staple, but a person could take it out and put it much in the same state that it was in before.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. Have you the copper here? A. This is it ( produced)—I did not know how much there was in the place—probably I never should have missed any, but this had been the cover of a roof—this is a very common form for old copper to be in—Dowding has been in my service seven or eight years—I had a very hight opinion of him.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did Hillesley drive the cart? A. Yes—he lived with his family, at Balham-hill—he has been bailed, and has surrendered to take his trial.
JAMES MATTHEWS. I am a marine-store dealer, of Wandaworth. On 30th March, Hillesley came to my house a little after nine o'clock, and offered me this piece of copper for sale—I asked where he got it—he said it was no matter; there it was, if I chose to buy it—I said it would not suit me, he had better take it away again—he took it away—I informed the police.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Had you ever seen him before? A. No—I am positive he is the man—I swear to the copper by some red paint on it.
ALFRED SPICE ( policeman, V 41.) I received information from Mr. Matthews, and on 6th April went to a stable on Mr. Loat's premises with him and the sergeant, and this copper was found there—I afterwards took
Hillesley—I asked whether he knew anything about the copper—he said he knew nothing about anything—I afterwards showed him the copper—Mr. Loat told him he had better tell the truth—something was said, but I did not hear what.
ROBERT EMERSON ( police-sergeant, 9 V.) I found this copper on 6th of April—I spoke to Hillesley about it—Mr. Loat told him he had better tell the truth—I waited till Dowding came—Mr. Loat said to him, "I have been informed some copper has been taken from my premises, and you know something about it"—Dowding said, "By whom have you been informed"—Mr. Loat said, "By Hillesley"—Dowding said, "Bring him before me" Mr. Loat said, "He is safe in custody"—Dowding stood a minute or five, and then said, "If I tell the truth, will you forgive me?"—Mr. Loat said, "I have nothing to do with it"—Dowding stood a second or so and said, "I had better tell you the truth; I will show you how I got it"—he went to the shed door, unscrewed the staple and the lock came off; you could then open the door.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. Were you in uniform? A. Yes; I had seen Dowding before, but had no conversation with him—I heard nothing said to him before about unscrewing the lock—I had no suspicion that that was the way it had been done—nothing had been said to Dowding about the staple till he went to it.
(The prisoners received good characters, and a witness engaged to take Hillesley into his service.)
HILLESLEY — GUILTY.— Recommended to mercy— Confined One Month.
DOWDING — GUILTY.— Recommended to mercy— Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Common Serjeant.

[trialAccount t18480515-1443]
1443. ANTHONY DONALDSON , stealing 1 half-sovereign; the moneys of James Robison Cockburn, his master.
MR. METCALFE conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES ROBISON COCKBURN. I am a pawnbroker, at Richmond—the prisoner was my shopman for two years—it was his duty to take in pledges, deliver them, and do the general business—when he took in this watch he had to examine it, and to lend according to his judgment, to make out two duplicates; one for the person pledging, and the other he puts on the article—before giving the money, he enters it in the auction book—he gives the money without coming to me—he had marked 1 l. on the ticket; he would advance 19 s. 10 d., as we are entitled to 2 d. on the ticket—he would enter in the auction book the name of the person, whether a housekeeper or a lodger, and the amount lent, 1 l., to correspond with the duplicate—on Saturday, 8th April, I found this ticket for 1 l. in the till, the time of pawning being on 3rd May, and on 8th April redeemed—I said to the prisoner, "You have charged more interest than you ought; you have charged 4 s. on the back"—he said, "It is all right"—I said, "I suppose it is; you have only lent 1 l. on it"—twelvemonth's interest would he 4 s.—the interest on 1 l. 10 s. would be
5 s. 8 d.—by my finding the ticket in the till, it would have come in that day—he had been serving that day in the shop and he must have taken it—I afterwards examined the auction book—the amount entered there was 1 l. 10 s., as if he had lent 1 l. 10 s. on it—the date in the book was 3rd May, 1847, and from the entries I saw below it, and before it, it must have been entered on that date—on the Monday evening I went to my cash book, and I found for that day's entry but a very few halfpence in excess, and had he entered 1 l. 10 s., and charged on the ticket only 1 l., there would have been an excess of 10 s.—I have, certainly, never received that 10 s.—I must have known if this 10 s. had been in excess—he lent 1 l. in May, 1847, which was paid to me in April, 1848, and in May, 1847, he credits his account in the book with 1 l. 10 s., whereas he only paid 1 l.
FRANCIS BREWER. I am a tailor, and live opposite Mr. COckburn, at Richmond. On Tuesday, 11th April, the prisoner came over to my shop—he asked me if I would allow him to burn some old letters and old books—I told him to go in the back kitchen, and he was in the act of tearing out some leaves and throwing them in the fire—he said his master suspected he had been robbing him, but he should not see the books—he said he should be imprisoned for what he had been doing, but he did not care for that—he said he should go out the back way—I said no, he came in the front way and he should go out that way—I then told the prosecutor.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18480515-1444]
1444. ANTHONY DONALDSON was again indicted , for embesslement.
MR. METCALFE conducted the Prosecution.
FREDERICK GAYDON. I am assistant to Mr. Loden, a watch and clock maker, in Blackfriars-road. A little before Christmas, 1846, I pawned two pairs of trowsers at different times, at Mr. Cockburn's; one pair for 12 s. or 14 s., and the other for 8 s.—about Christmas, 1847, I sent 23 s. to redeem them, by a post-office order, payable to the prisoner—my trowers were sent by the Parcel's Delivery Company in three or four days afterwards—some time afterwards I met the prisoner in London; I asked him what money there was deficient—he said 9 d. or 1 s. 9 d., and I paid it him—he had written to me about the post office order, and said it was 9 d. or 1 s. 9 d. deficient, and when I met him I paid it—he said he received the order; it was all right—I sent the duplicates with the order—I made the order payable to the prisoner because I had been in Mr. Cockburn's service, and did not wish him to know I pawned the things with the prisoner.
JAMES ROBISON COCKBURN. The prisoner was in my service—the book in which this money should have been entered as paid in 1846 is destroyed—I have examined my books—it is not entered as received at Christmas, 1847—I have not received the Post-office order—if these trowsers were desired by the witness to he sent home by the Parcels Delivery Company, it would have been the prisoner's duty to have entered them in this parcel-delivery book—here are my entries, Mrs. Cockburn's, and the prisoner's, in it—here is no entry of this parcel being sent—it would be the prisoner's duty to enter the parcel, for my protection—if it had been lost I should have been exonerated, because I sent it by the party that they had mentioned—I am sure the prisoner never accounted to me for this.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. Will you undertake to say that the money was never paid you? A. To the best of my belief, it never was—I will swear it was never accounted for to me—no parcel was ever
allowed to be sent out without being entered in this parcel-delivery book—here are two entries of 12 s. and 9 s.—without having the books, which are destroyed, it is impossible for me to tell what goods they refer to—the trowsers were pawned for 14 s. and 8 s.—on 11th Dec., here is entered 2 s. 1 d. interest, and 12 s. principal—I should say this is not the entry of the trowsers, because the witness says they were 14 s. and 8 s.—I know nothing about the taking them out.
STEPHEN CREED ( policeman, V 200.) I took the prisoner—I found this letter in his box—it is signed, "Frederick Gaydon"—( read)—"To Mr. Donaldson, at Mr. Cockburn's, Richmond, Surrey. 16, Blackfriars-road, Oct. 14, 1847. Dear Donaldson, I herewith inclose you a Post-office order for 23 s. If you will be kind enough to make those two pairs of trowsers in a paper-parcel, and send it by the London Parcel Delivery Company as soon as possible, you will oblige. If it comes to more than I have sent, please let me know."
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18480515-1445]
1445. ANTHONY DONALDSON was again indicted for stealing 1 cigarscase, value 10 s., the goods of James Robison Cockburn, his master.
MR. O'BRIEN conducted the Prosecution.
STEPHEN CREED ( policeman V 200.) I produce this cigar-case—I found it in the prisoner's box, on 19th April, in his room, at the top of Mr. Cockburn's house.
JAMES ROBISON COCKBURN. This cigar-case was part of my stock—I am not quite clear when I saw it last—I think a month or six weeks before it was found it was put into the trade stock for the prisoner to sell—it was not sold, or we should have it entered in this sale-book—( produced)—the selling price is half-a-guinea—it was never reported to me as having been sold.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. Will you undertake to swear you saw it within the last six months? A. I cannot say—my stock is large—there may be things that I have not seen for two or three years—I am confident this has not been sold since I gave it the prisoner to sell—I have a jeweller's shop—I sometimes bring things from there to the sale-shop—if the sale-shop pays for them there would be no entry of them—this book contains the amount of things entered—I suppose we do not sell 1 l. worth in three months without entering them—it might be that no entry was made of this article—the prisoner never paid me any money for it—he said two or three people had looked at it, and asked the price—part of it is silver—I do not know that I told him so—he has had no conversation with me about purchasing it—I never said it was valued at 4 s.—I have lost upwards of 300 l.
GUILTY. — Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18480515-1446]
1446. JOHN BATEMAN , stealing 1 gun, value 10 l., the goods of William Bereford.
MR. O'BRIEN conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM PERKINS. I am a gamekeeper to Mr. Gooding, of Breestock Lodge, Northamptonshire. I have had letters in the prisoner's name which I have reason to believe were not written by him—in Sept. last, I received a gun and a letter in the same writing as that I used to receive from the prisoner—in about a week I received a letter signed J. Bateman, telling me to take care of the gun that was sent down—I sent back a letter to know how he came by it—he wrote saying he had it from John Mays—this is it.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. How long have you had it? A.
About eight months. I am a relation of the prisoner's—I have destroyed the letter—he said he had it from John Mays for a debt due from him for board and lodging—he did not say it was to be kept till Mays paid him—it looked like a woman's writing—the prisoner cannot write, unless he has learned since he has been in London.
WILLIAM BERESFORD. I live in the Temple. In June last I had a gun which was removed with other things, from my residence at Islington, to my brother's warehouse in Tooley-street—it was missed about Sept.—this is it.
JOSEPH SUTTON. In June las I was removing Mr. Beresford's goods with the prisoner and four others—I moved what I believe was a gun in a wollen case—I saw it in the warehouse in Tooley-street—I saw it put away—the prisoner was there.
DANIEL BROWN. I am in the service of Marcus Bereford, of Pickle-herring wharf—the goods were removed there in June—a gun was brought from the van to me—I saw it in the warehouse—the prisoner was employed there up to the time he was charged, and had access to it—it was missed about three months afterwards—Mr. Beresford asked me, I said I knew nothing of it—he called the prisoner and asked him, he said he knew nothing of it.
Cross-examined. Q. He had been employed a long time at the wharf? A. Yes; Mays had been employed there, but was not then—I don't think I have seen him these six months—I have heard that he is dead, that he was starved and could not get employ.
THOMAS WEST ( policeman.) I took the prisoner, he said he knew nothing of the gun—I went to Northamptoushire and found it.
GUILTY. Aged 28.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18480515-1447]
1447. WILLIAM PORTSMOUTH , stealing 16lbs., weight of solder, value 10 s. and 49lbs. weight of lead, 6 s.; the goods of Edward Hoile and another, his masters.
JOSEPH WONFER. I live at No. 5, Caledonian-cottages, Battersea. I have one partner, named Edward Hoile—the prisoner was in my service—we were making alterations—we had a quantity of lead on our premises and factory.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. Did you miss any? A. No—it was impossible—the prisoner was brought back and searched in my presence—this lead was found inside his trowsers—he said, "Pray forgive me"—he has been several years in service in that house—he was there when we took the premise—I believe this to be our property.
ALFRED HARDEMAN ( policeman, V 73.) I took the prisoner. I found this piece of solder in his side pocket, and these forty-nine pounds of lead buttoned in the flap of his trowsers—this cord went round his neck to hold it up.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. — Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18480515-1448]
1448. JOHN FINCH and JAMES GOSBELL , stealing 4 sacks, and 2 quarters of malt, value 6 l. 4 s., the goods of William Jupp and others, the masters of Gosbell, he having been before convicted.
MR. PARRY conducted the Prosecution.
MATTHEW HENRY COWELL. I am in partnership with William Jupp and Mr. Wilks. We are brewers at St. George's-road, Southwark—Gosbell was in
our employ—on Friday 12th May we were to receive twenty-five quarters of malt in fifty sacks, from Mr. Jupp—Finch came with it—he was in Mr. Jupp's service, the malster—he brought this receipt and delivery not after, as I supposed the malt was delivered—Finch and Gosbell came into the counting-house—I asked Finch whether he had delivered twenty-five quarter—he said, "Yes"—I asked Gosbell whether he had received twenty-five quarters—he said, "Yes"—I then signed the receipt note and gave it to Finch—I afterwards received a communication from Newton, went into the granary and counted the sacks; there were but forty-six—I then followed the prisoners and found them in a public house—I asked Finch what he had got in his wagon, he said, two quarters of malt—he said, he hoped he had not done wrong and should not get himself into a scrape, but our man Gosbell ordered him to take them somewhere else—I told him to draw round—Gosbell went into the biewery—I sent for an officer and took him—he came—I asked him how it was—he said he thought the whole twenty-five quarters were delivered.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. When Finch said he had delivered the twenty-five quarters, if was the quantity he had delivered to Gosbell. A. Yes; Gosbell was the party to receive malt—Finch might have supposed that Gosbell had authority to dispose of the two quarters—I have heard an excellent character of Finch.
THOMAS NEWTON. I am in the employ of Jupp and Co. On Friday, 12th May, I saw twenty-three quarters delivered—Finch was in the wagon—he had got the next sack ready, the mount of it in his hand, waiting for the chain of the crane to come down; but instead of the chain, Gosbell came down—I asked why he did not pull the other two quarters up—he said, "That is going to the railway"—he went out to Finch—I told my master directly. THOMAS BAILEY. I am foreman to William Jupp, a maltster. On 12th May I gave Finch twenty-five quarters of malt to deliver to Mr. Cahell—if was his duty to deliver fifty sacks—when he left the wharf I saw no more of him.
Cross-examined. Q. How long has Finch been in your service? A. Seven years—he has delivered thousands of quarters—I never knew anything against him—his master will take him back directly.
ALFRED WAKEFIELD ( policeman, L 117.) I took Gosbell—he said he thought he had delivered it all.
( MR. METCALFE, on behalf of Gosbell, submitted that the property was never in the prosecutor's possession; MR. PARRY contended that the receipt by the servant was that of the master, and the COURT so held.)
GEORGE TAYLOR ( police-sergeant, L 29.) I produce a certificate of Gosbell's former conviction—( read—Convicted May, 1845, and confined six weeks)—he is the man.
FINCH — NOT GUILTY.
GOSBELL — GUILTY. Aged 38. Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18480515-1449]
1449. JESSE JAMES BURDETT, GEORGE WALLER , and ELIZABETH ELLIS , stealing 41 numbers of the London Journal, value 3 s. 5 d.; the goods of Henry Barrington Francis and others; Waller having been before convicted.
HENRY BARRINGTON FRANCIS. I am in partnership with my two sons. Last Saturday evening, at half-past nine o'clock, I came home and went into my shop—the prisoners were brought in—I missed forty-one numbers of the London Journal —they were brought in with the prisoners, and were our property.
THOMAS WEST ( policeman, M 249.) About nine o'clock last Saturday I saw the prisoners in company in Old Kent-road—I followed them to Mr. Francis's in Alfred-place—Waller passed and repassed the window—I went on, came back, and saw Burdett leave the shop-door, and place these numbers in Waller's apron—I stopped them—Ellis said, "They are ours"—Waller threw them from his apron, and Ellis ran off with them—I pursued and took her—Burdett was brought back—he said he would pay for them.
Burdett's Defence. I went in, and asked the young man if he had got any old numbers; he said, "Yes," I could have them for a shilling.
WILLIAM JENNINGS ( policeman, M 273.) I produce a certificate of Waller's former conviction—( read—Convicted Dec., 1846, and confined two Months)—he is the man.
* BURDETT — GUILTY. Aged 19.— Confined Six Months.
** WALLER — GUILTY. Aged 21.— Transported for Seven Years.
ELLIS — GUILTY. Aged 19.— Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18480515-1450]
1450. THOMAS CRAWLEY, JOHN WILLSHER , and WILLIAM RUTT , stealing 1 handkerchief, value 3 s.; the goods of William Johns, from his person.
WILLIAM JOHNS. I live at Kennington. On 28th April, about half-past eight o'clock in the evening, I was in Walworth-road—I felt my handkerchief taken out of my pocket—I turned, and the prisoners were behind me—I charged Rutt with having taken it—while I was speaking, West came and took him.
Rutt. Q. Did I go away? A. No.
THOMAS WEST ( policeman, M 249.) I was in Westminster-road between seven and eight o'clock that evening—I saw the prisoner Crawley try a lady's pocket, Willsher was covering him—they went on and attempted another pocket, but did not succeed—they went on to the Walworth-road—Crawley put his hand into a gentleman's pocket, and took out something white—I asked if he had lost anything—he said, "A white handkerchief," but he should not know it—in about ten minutes Mr. Johns passed—the prisoners followed him about 200 yards—Willsher stepped into the road, just by a stall—this handkerchief, which Mr. Johns claims, was found there—I went up, and found Rutt in conversation with Johns, who said, "They; have just picked my pocket"—I took Rutt and Willsher—Rutt said, "I know nothing of that man," meaning Willsher.
THOMAS TOURESEY ( policeman, M 237.) I was watching the prisoners—I saw what West has stated—they were all trying pockets.
MARTHA EASTER. I wa sitting at my stall about half-past eight o'clock that evening—I saw a handkerchief lying—I picked it up and gave it to West.
† CRAWLEY — GUILTY. Aged 19.— Confined Six Months.
†* WILLSHER — GUILTY. Aged 20.— Transported for Seven Years.
† RUTT — GUILTY. Aged 20.— Confined One Year.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, JUNE 12 TH, 1848.
